> Professor, thank you.  
>   
> On Game theory, which I have been studying for years, is a dynamic that I have not heard you discuss yet, that is:   
>   
> Information asymmetry and violence seem to be inversely proportional.   
>   
> In games where information is equal (chess) the game is strictly strategic and power is determined by processing power and the level of force that can be applied. Outcomes can be determined and measured to a degree: Behavior is dictated strictly by the outcome desired. (Going all in can be safe/rational, violence is high/likely)  
>   
> Whereas in asymmetric information (poker, MAD), much of the game is about leveraging information, negotiation, wits and bluffing: Not knowing what the opponent might know massively tempers behavior (going all in = risky/irrational, violence is low/less likely).

> In the end everything is necessarily flawed, this is what makes the foil, the edge, the contrast useful for analysis, and also at the same time all these ideas will be misinterpreted and many false assumptions will be built in and people will argue with one another assuming that the other is using the same frame of reference, and the same assumptions or presumptions.  Chomsky talked about presumptions and how important they are.  
  
> 
  
> Marx said that religion was the evil of the world, but he was taken far too literally, and no one ever asked- what are the religions that hide in plain sight?  The worship of power and money and to some extent depravity and exploitation is what pervades all religions.  BUT at least actual religions in most cases have assumed that there is such a thing as good and evil...and there is such a thing as what is the right thing to do.   So they don't debate whether Jesus for example should have whipped the money changers in the temple.  They just say oh yeah of course he did, and it was the only time he was said to have been driven into a rage. ...
  
> 
  
> Anyways consider the idea of Liberalism and Liberty.  If it is the capacity for learning critical thinking, and the freedom to read and learn about  literature and art in all of its historical context where there are no sacred cows, where we can question everything in good faith with true intellectual honesty and curiosity then that is I think what is liberty and liberalism to some extent.  Sure people try to say that everything is all about "markets", but when we focus too much on money and the value of stuff being measured only in terms of money then we end up with a more neoliberal drift.  We end up with a world where no one can afford anything, no one is interested in reading anything, no one is interested in questioning anything and everyone is trapped in a cycle of consumerism, many are homeless, labor is not valued, the rent is too high, no one owns anything except for the extreme few who seem to own way too much.
  
> 
  
> And like in the US for example I can complain about this all day long, but the fact and reality of an unfree, antiliberal society will remain.  I can go and vote in the elections that they hold, but like George Carlin said the elections are just the illusion of choice, the popular vote is a minority consensus vote and the vote is being counted and managed in privately owned data black boxes.  And even if the cream was to rise to the top or some diamond was made in the rough and that savior like person rose to power, they would be quickly and easily coopted and blackmailed into doing many evil things which are detrimental to the many and to the whole group eventually.
  
> 
  
> This is the problem with greed culture and neoliberalism and shock doctrine.  The worship of evil is even bad for those who revel in and benefit from it, and I don't think it even creates diamonds from pressure.  It doesn't really even have a silver lining to it.  Because a lot of what is great about the world is often not even something we knew was there when it was there, and we have a hard time talking about it when it was gone, we pretend like it never existed or we didn't know we even had it until it was gone.  The spirit I guess and the culture is priceless when people are free and have liberty and liberalism- when they are free to have curiosity in good faith and are intellectually honest etc. 
  
> 
  
> When Karl Marx was talking about religion, maybe what he should have critiqued was magical thinking, and how powerful it is to have to meet people in the middle when they willfully or subconsciously take on a great many unnecessary or useless contradictions and false presumptions.  And that middle ground is always unfair- it trumps reason and understanding.  Especially when a great many people believe it.  And that is also what I think the greater meaning of the gospel is- it is ultimately an argument for agnosticism guided by good faith, trust in one another, and love for one another.

> Capitalism is an economic organization which facilitates trade via Private Property, where Private entities own the means of production.  
>   
> Socialism is an economic organization in which Property is Personal instead of Private, and the people of a given country have majority say over the means of production in the form of some sort of governing institution.   
>   
> Communism is a societal structure in which there is no longer a need for Money, Class, or Nationality because systems of distribution have become so efficient.   
>   
> Capitalist Economic Policy and Socialist Economic Policy can be interpreted to exist on a sliding scale, but Communism is a completely separate idea that speaks to more than just economics. > Over the past twenty years, China has become Africa’s largest trading partner and top bilateral creditor, investing heavily in infrastructure, mining, and energy. While this has spurred development, several critical concerns have emerged:  
>   
> Resource Extraction and Trade Imbalance  
> China primarily imports African raw materials—especially oil, copper, cobalt, and other critical minerals—while exporting manufactured goods. This has led to an imbalanced trade relationship, with African economies remaining dependent on commodity exports. Critics argue this model resembles neo-colonial exploitation, as it limits industrialization and value addition within Africa.   
>   
> Debt-Trap Diplomacy  
> China has loaned over $150 billion to African governments since 2000.  Countries like Angola, Ethiopia, Zambia, and Kenya now struggle with debt sustainability.  Projects are often secured against future resource revenues, raising fears of "debt-trap diplomacy," where strategic assets could be seized or leveraged for political influence if repayments fail.   
>   
> Labor and Human Rights Abuses  
> Chinese-run mining and construction projects have been linked to poor labor conditions, including:  
>   
> Low wages and delayed payments  
> Use of Chinese labor instead of local workers  
> Reports of forced labor and human trafficking, such as the 2019 Johannesburg case where Malawians were exploited in slave-like conditions  
> Child labor in cobalt mines in the Democratic Republic of the Congo   
> Workers in Zimbabwe and the DRC have reported physical abuse and threats for speaking up.  
>   
> Environmental Damage  
> Chinese mining operations have caused significant environmental harm, including:  
>   
> Deforestation and habitat destruction  
> Water contamination from mercury and mine tailings (e.g., in the Central African Republic)  
> Inadequate environmental impact assessments and weak enforcement of safeguards   
> Infrastructure with Strings Attached  
> While China has built roads, railways, and ports, many projects:  
>   
> Use Chinese firms and labor, limiting local job creation and skills transfer  
> Serve Chinese strategic interests, such as securing transport routes for resources  
> Are criticized for "shoddy" construction and lack of transparency, leading some countries (like Kenya and Ghana) to cancel contracts   
> Political Influence  
> China leverages economic ties for geopolitical gains. African support in international forums—such as on issues related to Taiwan or the South China Sea—is often seen as a return on investment.  This has raised concerns about sovereignty and policy independence

> Five minutes of video, and it becomes clear what the indiscriminate use of Game Theory leads to. It pushes its users to see everything in black and white. On the other hand, this Manichean superficiality leads to the disregard of factors hidden beneath the appearance of phenomena.
  
> 
  
> It's hard to believe the author made such errors after so many classes teaching not to do so. Without prejudice to a React video (and perhaps an article) I will make on @canalmultipolartv, I will state from the outset that the analysis fails, at the very least and to start with, in the following conceptual fallacies:
  
> 
  
> Lack of understanding of the concept of "mode of production" and the distinction between "mode of production" and the resulting politico-social superstructure, as well as the role the mode of production plays in social construction.
  
> 
  
> Unaware of the difference between Marxism and Leninism and the role of Lenin and Leninism in the political organization of a society transitioning from capitalism (in the Russian case, still largely feudal) to socialism.
  
> 
  
> The role of the "communist party" as an instrument and not an end in itself, and as an instrument for defending a transition, which may later even dispense with its own existence (Lenin even speaks of dispensing with the state itself once class contradictions are overcome);
  
> 
  
> Capitalism as the antithesis of Communism only exists in the author's head; at most, for Marx, the antithesis of capitalism is socialism, with communism being a synthesis, in the Hegelian sense, of the confrontation between two transitional modes of production (for Marx, Capitalism and Socialism are transitional modes of production, while communism is terminal); Communism is the result of "overcoming" [Aufhebung], meaning it is "superior," just as socialism is the result of "overcoming" capitalism;
  
> 
  
> The author is thus unaware of materialist dialectics and the role of historical materialism, that is, the way modes of production succeed each other as a result of their internal and external contradictions, overcoming themselves in new, historical (i.e., resulting from the material conditions of a specific era, historical phase), transient modes of production, with superior modes prevailing over the overcome ones;
  
> 
  
> The author is also unaware that for Marx, each material context is historical, meaning it is transient, and therefore, the very existence of a vanguard, of a communist party, is also historical, existing only while material reality demands it, extinguishing itself, like everything in nature, when it is no longer needed; hence, each material context is specific to the reality in which it exists: there is not one single communism, just as there is not one single feudalism, one single capitalism...
  
> 
  
> Unaware of Marx's life, Marxism, and that Marx spoke very little of "communism." Marx spoke mainly about capitalism, so that it could be known and understood, so that, gaining consciousness, the proletariat could use its contradictions to overcome it;
  
> 
  
> Also unaware that for Marx, capitalism is an evolution, that modes of production evolve by overcoming themselves, but, to the extent that some are syntheses of the contradictions of previous ones ("synthesis" and not "contradiction"), they retain remnants of the previous ones, meaning it is a construction by addition and substitution, and not merely by exclusion.
  
> 
  
> These are some profound contradictions that are serious and demonstrate grave failures in the structure of thought (even a lack of understanding of how dialectics works). But in the superficial contradictions, the matter is also serious:
  
> 
  
> a) Marx did not invent "communism"; communism is an ancient human aspiration, dating back to prehistoric humans and means "living in community, sharing everything, including power and property." Examples of what can be communism (humanity only lived in communism in its primitive form (and not "socialism" as the author says), meaning without consciousness of doing it). Thomas More in Utopia, Jesus Christ, and many others before Marx established principles and functioning that we could consider "communist," in light of progressive political thought;
  
> b) The author is unaware of the difference between "scientific socialism" and "utopian socialism," hence the confusion regarding Marx. Marx founded the scientific socialist current, meaning the one that would use the most advanced scientific knowledge to accelerate the overcoming of capitalism and arrive more quickly at socialism and then communism. Superficially speaking (it's much more than this).
  
> c) The author says Marx was an instrument of capitalism because England was "the most capitalist" and let him live there. Unaware that Marx also lived in Germany and was only expelled because he did not follow the imposed rules. But, if England was "the most capitalist," what about it being a Monarchy, which the author says is "an enemy of capitalism"? And what about the USA, which in the 20th century are the most capitalist, and imprisoned all communists? Or that 20th-century fascism was based on private capitalist monopolies, and imprisoned communists? Or that, in the most capitalist era of all, the present one, many socialist parties govern, not just being tolerated (like some communists), but supported by capitalism? Just to directly attack some of the author's superficial hypotheses.
  
> d) The author is unaware of the relationship between "mode of production," the politico-social superstructure that protects it, and how these transition within modes of production. Constitutional monarchy is an adaptation to the capitalist mode of production. Therefore, we can have capitalism and monarchy and, in theory, even socialism and monarchy.
  
> e) The author is unaware of the difference between nationalism and patriotism and internationalism. Internationalism means "friendship between peoples, between workers" and not "abolition of borders." This utopian vision was never Marx's. There are no countries more sovereigntist than Cuba or North Korea, governed by communist parties, just like China. The role of the Communist Party in those countries is to ensure the evolutionary process towards socialism is carried out; they exist to defend and lead that trajectory, containing the political power of capitalism. In China, this capitalist power is contained through CPC committees in companies, public ownership of strategic sectors, etc... And it is also necessary to distinguish between "private property" and "capitalism," as they are not the same thing.
  
> f) In socialism, "democracy" is not achieved through voting, but through daily participation in political life. Socialist society is activated through social structures (unions, associations, committees (like soviets), cooperatives...), which have elected representatives and have political power. They are forms of socialization of property. Consider Huawei, impossible in a "capitalist" country, meaning a country where political initiative comes from capital and not from the worker). In capitalism, you vote every 4 years, in elections paid for by capitalism, to legitimize it.
  
> g) The author is unaware that democratic construction, like socialism, is very diverse, with many models and practices. In the case of Religion, what about Cuba? A very religious society, governed by a communist party? What about the Cuban constitution being the only one in the world that recognizes the right to change one's sex?
  
> h) The author is unaware that Marx died in misery and had two children die of hunger, being very poorly treated by the "capitalism" he supposedly served, and that Engels was disinherited by his family. And Lenin who exiled himself in Germany and not England?
  
> i) Unaware that in socialism (socialization of property) and in "communism" (collectivization of property), democracy is presumed; for Marx, the superior form of democracy is economic, and that is measured by the amount of wealth that goes to the working people. The levels of redistribution and wealth in the USSR were enormous and the social system was very good. Unions managed pensions, social security. The prices of fundamental things were very cheap. Public services in Vietnam, Cuba, China, Korea are very cheap. Because they are rights and not meant for accumulation.
  
> j) And here the author shows a lack of understanding of the function of property for Marx. Property has a social purpose, meaning to serve society. In capitalism, property has an economic purpose, of accumulation. The state exists to protect that accumulation, whether in its nationalist, globalist, monarchical, or theocratic form, as in Saudi Arabia. The socialist or communist state (there hasn't been one yet) aims to protect distribution and the social purpose of property. Since everything revolves around satisfying material needs, that satisfaction guaranteed by the superstructure (another concept the author is unaware of) is the superior form of democracy.
  
> 
  
> I caught all this in five minutes... What a disappointment with this author!

Marxist communism is essentially meant to be the conclusion of the cycle.

>

> **@anandboss7034**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=axqDLhWs93Q&lc=UgyRIQcZhXdHbwTXFiB4AaABAg.ASgqtL-Cg77AUIralV9LXT)  
>
> ​@xiaofeiqin184 you've make some interesting points. For starters, value as a theory. You've made the claim there is no objective value. I don't entirely disagree, but value is a very broad term, where as marx refers to a specific kind of value, 2 specific kinds really. Use value and exchange value. These values are specifically refering to the market for commodity goods.  
>   
> Marx defines use value as the qualitative value of a commodity. In other words what can it be used for, what is the benefit recieved from it.   
>   
> Exchange value is different. It is the equivication of all commodities into a quantitative unit of measure.   
>   
> In order to trade, it is necessary to first accept the idea that certain amount of one thing is equal to a certain amount of another.   
>   
> But where does this Exchange value actually come from. Marx attributes it primarily to human expenditure. I do not entirely agree with Marx on this, but I do think there is a certain amount of truth to this.   
>   
> It's easiest to explain through free market perfect competition. In perfect competition, the price of a commodity will inevitably fall to its cheapest viable price. But why is it unable to fall below this? Primarily it's a time issue. If the commodity is to cheap you'll end up with a shortage. Why? Because there's a certain amount of time required to produce more. And time can is something human beings expend.   
>   
> Regardless of the form of labour, there is a certain amount of required for value to be generated.  
>   
> But Marx isn't concerned with the individual amount of time spent by each individual, because in a market there is incentive to producing faster.  
>   
> So the value of commodity is the average time required to create said commodity.   
>   
> The reason why singers and artists of all kinds are able to generated such extreme amounts of value, is because they sell a unique commodity. They have a monopoly in the market for there art, and thus they have a large amount of control over the average production time for their work.  
>   
> It's important to note, that "commodity" denotes a product in demand. Not everything produced is commodity, the product must be of value to other people. Its use value must be recognized by others. Marx acknowledges this from the beginning.   
>   
> So the theory is not as simple as "labour = value" as you to assume.

> You should distinguish between a monarchy in its height and a monarchy in decline. For starters, read Plato's Republic, specifically Book 8. A fair distribution of wealth doesn't apply to the poor class or the enslaved population, but only to the elite who own property and their own income. A monarch can purge their elite, redistribute property, and elevate people in status if they trust their guard. A monarch has no interest in elevating a portion of the elite and rapidly impoverishing the majority, as this undermines their power. Meanwhile, the people in the monarchy live their ordinary lives without rights or authority. Capitalism inevitably absorbs small and medium-sized property owners, leading to monopolies or, at best, cartelism. Fewer property owners lead to increased exploitation of the people, their impoverishment, and enslavement. Therefore, yes, a monarchy is, in some ways, more fair than capitalism.

Charity does indeed redistribute "wealth" in the vernacular sense, as does the welfare state. The bourgeois system does forgive debts occasionally also. I'm not stating this to defend that system, but to point out the speaker is incorrect when he claims that monarchies have these features and capitalism does not. Monarchy, capitalism and socialism exist on a spectrum in real life and often adopt features in common with one another other (if for no reason but pragmatism - e.g. Keynesian economics in capitalism, or Lenin's NEP), so I find his statements simplistic and take objection to the lack of acknowledgement that capitalist and communist ideologies have changed since their early forms (e.g. no country ever followed what some people call orthodox marxism - and contrary to his statements, communist leaders occasionally embraced nationalism rather than opposed it)

[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=axqDLhWs93Q&lc=UgzdaKSHh05BvxCsjYp4AaABAg)

Bolsheviks carried out train robberies as part of their “expropriations” to raise money for revolutionary work against the Russian Tsar. These actions happened mainly between 1905 and 1907. The group needed cash for arms, printing newspapers, and hiding party members. Kote Tsintsadze led several raids, including one on a train at Kars and another ambush on a gold train in Chiatura that carried 21,000 rubles in miners’ wages. ￼ Stalin helped organize these efforts while Lenin approved them in secret, even after a 1907 party congress banned such tactics. ￼ The robberies brought in funds that kept the Bolshevik faction active during crackdowns after the 1905 revolution failed.
These heists showed how communists adapted Marx’s idea of taking back wealth from capitalists through direct force. Bolsheviks saw banks and transports as tools of the rich, so robbing them counted as reclaiming for the workers. The money bought weapons and supported strikes, which built support for the 1917 takeover. ￼ But the violence killed civilians and split socialists; Mensheviks called it banditry and pushed to stop it. ￼ Later, Chinese communists under Mao Zedong copied this with a 1927 train robbery of a bank shipment to fund their fight. ￼ Overall, the heists provided quick cash that helped small communist groups survive and grow into governments.

> You should check out Branko Milanovic on this relation between nationalism, communism & industrialization. He explains the actually-existing Socialist & Communist societies as primarily modernizers -- and if you study the history of China, Yugoslavia, Russia or know people from there who are only two or three generations out of feudal poverty, they often have familial knowledge of that  
>   
> The Socialist & Communist movements that actually came to power came to power on the peripheries of capitalism, not the core -- and those Parties organized both a material revolution of bringing industrial production & scientific knowledge (like medicine) that already existed to places that didn't yet have it -- and also abolishing oppressive old social practices that were freezing progress, ranging from relations of landownership to gendered abuse like foot-binding  
>   
> Often the nationalist movements in those regions were allies against outside, more developed capitalist powers, but they could also be enemies & rivals. Milanovic points out the Communist Parties that did take power & held onto it for a few generations *were* the modernizing & industrializing class that transformed these societies and, like you say, achieved actual national sovereignty as part and parcel of that modernizing project  
>   
> What the Professor says around 12:00 here, in contrast, is all wrong -- the facts are often blurred (it's Lenin, not Marx or Engels, who developed the Party & vanguard ideas; Engels was happy with electoralism, like most Marxists were before 1917); but the grand picture is even more wrong: the Socialism that actually existed did *not* emerge from industrial development -> socialism, it emerged in these peripheral, feudal countries from socialism -> industrial development, as a conscious & intentional political project of these Communist parties (who typically achieved power by being the most popular & successful organized force on the ground in already-existing civil wars, interestingly; overcoming the nationalists, who typically became quisling forces for the Axis)

>
> ​@talenlunari5114 The class conflict cannot be both a complete fiction AND a lens. A lens determines where you're focusing your attention, which is not a matter of truth or falsehood. The economic class divide between owners and laborers is an observable tension over who gets what relative proportion of surplus value in the cycle of production. Again, the history of labor unions is directly about this. Workers want to be paid for their labor, and owners want to be paid for their property.  

> **@talenlunari5114**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=axqDLhWs93Q&lc=Ugyqg6AoQ6SnjOaj6PR4AaABAg.ASeOBveTbkLAUIbn3PkEaN)  
>
> @mavortius8388 The lens is an ideology in which you interpret events. If I observe a tree bending in the wind and claim that the tree is doing its morning calisthenics I am interpreting the event through my (incorrect) lens. Surplus value does not exist. It is an outside interpretation of the dynamics of markets. Wages are the price an employer is willing to pay for an employee's labor, and is subject to the same laws of supply and demand as any other good or service. The history of labor unions is a history of price fixing above market levels and the creation of more unemployment. We have emphirical studies that confirm across OECD countries from 1960-1996 that greater labor union involvement significantly increased unemployment for the young and old populations and only benefitted the prime-aged labor force.  

> **@mavortius8388** | 👍 4 likes  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=axqDLhWs93Q&lc=Ugyqg6AoQ6SnjOaj6PR4AaABAg.ASeOBveTbkLAUIeC2rCQrx)  
>
> ​@talenlunari5114 Surplus value is just the value brought into an enterprise when the costs for production are lower than the prices paid for production. It's basically profit, and labor unions allow for workers to bargain collectively for a better share of the profit. That conflict of interests, which clearly exists between employers and workers, is the basis of class struggle. To be a member of a class is to share in the same interests of that class. The working class includes those who primarily work to meet their needs, while the owning class meets their needs primarily through ownership. You can cut reality into different sections than that if you like, but the lens is transparent for reality to show through.  

> **@talenlunari5114**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=axqDLhWs93Q&lc=Ugyqg6AoQ6SnjOaj6PR4AaABAg.ASeOBveTbkLAUIjkq4Miof)  
>
> @mavortius8388 Value is not measured in surplus because value is not intrinsic, objective, or predetermined in the good or service. Value is determined by the problem solving capacity of the good based off the subjective needs and wants of the market. Profit is the is the market reward for correctly anticipating consumer needs and shouldering the burden of risk in deploying and coordinating resources more efficiently than others would. Business owners are not part of a separate class, they are risk takers who are willing to convert their private property into tools of production. Workers who voluntarily sell their labor at the agreed upon wage price utilize the tools of their employer to elevate their own productivity and real earnings without the same amount of risk. These are not classes, but fluid roles that can be filled at different stages of an individual's social mobility. You are perfectly free to get a loan from a bank to start your own business if you are confident in your idea, and tens of thousands of regular people do exactly this every year. The marxist lens distorts a reality of mutual benefit and voluntary exchange and incorrectly splits them into antagonistic forces, its a false model of reality applied backwards to what it observes.

> 
  
> 6:20 China's own revolution was purposefully nationalist as a collaboration between different classes - peasants, workers, petty bourgeois, and national bourgeois. It's what the 4 stars on the Chinese flag represent. They had recently endured the century of humiliation and needed to repel any threat to their sovereignty from Britain, Japan, Russia, France, America. 20th century revolutions would clearly distinguish between reactionary and liberatory nationalism. It became clear in the 40s, 50s, and 60s that the former could be an effective rallying cry for socialist revolution and/or liberation from colonial subjugation in counties with material conditions favorable  for usage of such a strategy. In China this was taken at Mao's behest based on his analysis of his country's material conditions. I'd argue China is setting out to renew national pride in the face of threats from the US and also to confidently assert theirselves on the global stage as an alternative international partner (and potential global hegemon) to the US.

The rich quickly went back to getting richer by the 1950s and gutting workers' unions and pensions, brought to the next extreme by Reagan and Thatcher a couple decades later and returning to classical liberal economics.

民主这个概念原本就是属于共产党的，在几十年前，资本主义社会如果谁提倡民主，谁就是共产党，谁就是敌人。

导致这样的结果，主要是苏联倒台的原因，话语权丢失，没人扛得起这面旗帜了，而当时的中国，实在太穷了，没那个实力。

在美苏争霸的时候，或者更早的时候，资本主义阵营对社会主义阵营喊话的是资本主义这边是自由的。来吧，只要你有本事，你就可以实现你的抱负。而社会主义阵营向资本主义阵营喊话的是，社会主义阵营是民主的，团结的，照顾弱势的，帮你提升的。你要是觉得在资本主义社会生活的难受，不公，来我们社会主义阵营吧。

当时资本主义社会的提倡的是自由。 后来苏联解体了，以美国为首的资本主义社会就把自己的口号改为民主与自由了。 所以，说到底，这一切还都是国际话语权的问题。一旦中国将来崛起了，掌握国际话语权了，中国也可以把自己目前的“民主协商集中制”说成是真民主，同时把西方国家的选举投票党争说成是假民主。



中苏争霸的时期，社会主义就是以民主自居，大锅饭公社什么的都是民主平等的产物。资本主义在那个时候是以自由自居的，后来苏联解体后，民主这个词被资本主义重新定义变成了现在的“民主”，并且以美国来霸权解释民主的含义和抨击其他国家不符合其民主定义的地方，所以说的确以前我们就是民主，只不过是失败的民主，现在的民主是美式假民主，以后肯定还需要重新定义民主，不过重新定义肯定是在美国霸权之后了。我们这个年代的人一提到民主肯定想到的是投票，但是投票到底有没有用，有很多的书都有做说明，像是《以大制胜》《suicide of the west》《行为》等等等等。所以说历史上来讲中国（中华民国，中华人民共和国）是民主国家并不为过，只是“不符合”现在的定义，当然这个不符合到底有多不符合我也存疑，就以国外总说中国没有投票来说，中国当然有民主投票，只不过是阶梯性质的选人民代表，然后再以人民代表的形式选参政的人，形式和美国很相似只不过叫法不同而已。中国现在是越来越好，向着好的方向发展，民主自然需要重新定义。

### **@kyacchi3416** | 👍 52 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=axqDLhWs93Q&lc=UgyaKo_4AC_USPlnfSN4AaABAg)

I've disagreed with things you've said in the past, but never have I seen one of your lectures filled with so much misinformation I felt compelled to comment about it. I am not a Marxist - so I don't have any skin in the game saying this - but as someone that has read Marx, I feel like you owe it to your students and your million plus subscribers to actually take the time to properly educate yourself on the subject in question. Saying "I'm not saying this is true, but" is not circumventing the fact that you're making a lot of claims that are profoundly false and misleading if you hold them to any amount of scrutiny. I genuinely don't have the time to go through every remark in this video and refute them with credible sources to read further, but I'll at least list some issues as I watch.

- Communism is summarily a stateless, moneyless, classless society. That is diametrically opposed to capitalism at a foundational level. Methodologically, proposed transitions to communism may not exhibit this polarity, but it doesn't change the fact that asserting them as compatible is just an untrue statement.

- China's transition to a hybridized capitalist system at the behest of Xiaoping was considered a tool of necessity (despite criticism by many communists to this day) in order to build stability, especially wrt to US-employed economic sanctions and food shortages. It only makes sense that the transition was seamless on account of the CCP's lineage of Marxism-Leninism, enacting a variant of what Lenin himself called state capitalism. The same government enacted this change - there was no need for a violent transition back to capitalism.

- Communism being created by capitalism as a means of destroying capitalism's enemies doesn't hold water on so many levels, but for starters, communism predates capitalism by millennia (what Marx called primitive communism, though this is likely a misnomer bred from Enlightenment-derived racism). Furthermore, capitalist societies have been violently anti-communist since communism became a threat to ruling class hegemony. The death toll of communists and anarchists from anti-communist violence is literally countless. Deaths from the Holocaust? Economic sanctions? Red Scare? McCarthyism? Numerous executions throughout Europe and the Global South? Massacres in the US? CIA-backed coups? That doesn't come from an economic system that considers the Left a tool to maintain power.

- Religion is not an enemy of capitalism - like you've even stated in previous lectures, the propagation of monotheism is one of societal control - a tool that places money and individualism (two guiding facets of capitalist ideology) at the forefront of guiding principles for humanity. It is, in fact, extremely effective in the upkeep of power because God can be retooled at the whim of the state. The same goes for nationalism, a tool of the nation-state. While it contradictorily has been used as an anti-capitalist tool in the past (China itself and Korean anarchism being stand-out examples), it lends itself to exceptionalism, which aligns itself neatly with capitalism's tools of control. I highly recommend Fredy Perlman's The Continuining Appeal of Nationalism to learn more about how it came to be as a concept and how it's been used to instigate wars and violent forms of bigotry (all of which are respectively used to maintain power). The bourgeoisie are going to flow capital across borders regardless of prevailing sentiment over national pride.

- The rich paying more taxes does not equate to socialism. If anything, it's a singular facet of social democracy (which actually has something resembling democracy, though historically even that isn't always true). Socialism is a dictatorship of the proletariat - the workers own the means of production.

- Communism does not work with capitalism to destroy these things. It is opposed to capitalism, which upholds these facets - among others - as tools to maintain its status quo (arguably, communists are not even monolithically committed to ending religion). Monarchism is a separate ideology in conflict with both capitalism and communism.

- You do understand what a vanguard party is (specifically this was a concept put forth by Lenin well after communism was given academic context but I digress), but it is not tantamount to an oligarchy. An example of an oligarchy would be a wealthy elite, not a handful of bureaucrats reorganizing a centralized economy.

- "Religion is the opiate of the masses" is not claiming that religion is evil. In fact, Marx was softer on religion than many of his contemporaries - especially compared to the likes of Stirner.

- Marx was not the founder of communism. He even acknowledges in his writing that not only did he not come up with the idea, he didn't even put a name to it. He even acknowledges that it existed in other forms long before the term originated.

- 19th century Britain was a haven for protecting free speech and press; it wasn't uncommon for political exiles to end up there, Marx included. Despite not imposing censorship, Marx was not totally uncontested by British authorities. Marx earned most of his income through commercial publication. Surely, his books and translations made no money; his journalism did. Also, as you speculated, he did have several patrons. While Engels was one of these, he was not so at the behest of his father - a wealthy industrialist who correctly considered Engels a class traitor.

- Socialism was by no means the dominant ideology, though it was popular amongst the working class of industrial Europe due to observable exploitation under capitalism.

That's a lot of issues to pick up in only the first 10 minutes. When I have more time I can continue, but generally speaking I just want better from you, someone who's gained a lot of attention lately for their transparency and earnestness teaching high schoolers (who deserve a more grounded understanding of a subject that's shaped even their home country in such a profound way).

> @redcow78 I actually recognize that he is responding to communism "in practice". The problem with that notion is that communism is an ocean of thought and the methodological conception of "in practice" varies greatly depending on who you're talking to. Many communists do believe that a state is required for enforcing change, but communists are not a monolith. Marxist-Leninists may be the loudest breed, but this is not the only communist ideology, nor the only one in practice. The idea that communism is a stateless, moneyless, classless society is not contested; the method for transition to communism is. That is why I specifically said "Communism is diametrically opposed to capitalism at a foundational level. Methodologically, proposed transitions to communism may not exhibit this polarity".  
>   
> I will say though, exceedingly few self-proclaimed communists believe that enacting communism is tantamount to utopia. There are certainly the rare delusional types that haven't studied the politics they claim but that is not the majority. As for the state apparatus being suggestive to corruption, failing to recognize hierarchy's disposition towards this tendency has long been a subject of critique among communists - one need look no further than malcontents amongst communists in the Soviet Union who were quite vocal about this.  
>   
> All that being said, Prof. Jiang made plenty of bunk statements unrelated to the idea of how societies attempting at communism have panned out historically, making it evident he hasn't done his homework on the subject. The fact that he cites The Communist Manifesto - a largely useless document meant to stoke the revolutionary spirit in industrial Europe - as a source in the description reinforces that point. Not to mention the conspiratorial nonsense about communism being developed to aid capitalism, as if that makes the slightest bit of sense. He even literally says "Karl Marx is the founder of communism"; those words alone belie a lack of understanding of Marx, communism, and the context surrounding their academic throughline. This video even displays a shortsighted understanding of capitalism and how it's reinforced. I'm unconvinced he's interested in actually trying to learn because a lot of what he said was rather indefensible.

4:20 Wrong. Nationalism emerged together with capitalism in Europe 15th-16th century, and it was, in fact, an enemy of feudalism. So why then did communist regimes, on the contrary, strengthen nationalism? In North Korea or Albania, it was the nationalism of the dominant nation. In the USSR and China, national minorities were supported and their rights were strengthened.
7:10 How does this negate democracy? After all, communists become the vanguard and assume power thanks to the support of the population. Communist power does not arise out of nowhere. They have neither a divine calling nor vast capital, which attracts power like a magnet. There is no other way to gain power. The population offers its support and chooses communist candidates to express its will. 
8:00 You cannot cite isolated examples as a rule. History is full of far more examples of countries that did not become prosperous after transitioning from communism back to the capitalism.
11:05 Who said that? Why is the transition to socialism natural? Everything points to the opposite—to the strengthening of capitalism’s pressure on the working class and the consolidation of private capital’s power. South Korea proves this. Even the U.S. is quite telling in this regard. 
And the erosion of the middle class in Western countries in the 21st century is also an indicator of this trend.
14:00 It was precisely because of communism, as embodied by the USSR, had won World War II, that Western Europe began to embrace elements of socialism within themselves. It’s simply basic competition. Capitalism, as it stood, could not compete with the pace of rising prosperity in communist countries. And it is no coincidence that communist movements and parties grew strong in Europe, especially in Italy.
16:10 Poor? Ask people on the street if someone who owns an apartment or a house, has a free college degree, a family with several children whom the parents can support, a plot of land—often with a full-fledged country house (essentially a second home)— and the ability to go on vacation every year to health resorts, beautiful places, or even abroad - IS CONSIDERED POOR? And sometimes even have a family car (despite a well-developed public transportation network, which is completely unfamiliar to Americans and Canadians)! And free healthcare (which is almost on par with private healthcare in the West in terms of quality)
And all of the above—not on credit!!!!!!!!!!!!!!!
That’s what life of ordinary engineers, scientists, or factory workers in the USSR or the GDR were like!
Poverty is in your head. Poverty of knowledge. 

Marx spoke of social ownership of the means of production, or more precisely, ownership by the workers themselves who use these means.
27:43 Communism is the abolition of private ownership of the means of production. And land was the means of production for the majority of the population at that time. But this is not the goal; it is a means to an end. The goal is the abolition of large-scale private capital. Exactly the capital that, through the governments it controls, promotes wars against weaker countries(USA vs other world), tax breaks for big business(Margaret Thatcher, Javier Milei), and debt forgiveness at the expense of the state budget(assistance to private banks after 2008 crisis). And, of course, the restriction of rights and freedoms within its own country, for the sake of the system’s stability and the early identification of dangerous individuals and groups. who ready to change this system.
27:50 Hahahaha! How cleverly he skipped the second point! Yet there’s a logical inconsistency in with what the author said about the first point. What income should taxes be levied on if the abolition of private property is declared? Why have a progressive tax scale if there are no rich people? What a cheap trick by the lecturer.
27:55 Meanwhile, in non-communist modern Belgium and Spain, the inheritance tax can reach 80% in some cases. 
There isn’t even an attempt to understand the meaning of the manifesto’s point. A person shouldn’t have to rely on an inheritance to maintain their standard of living, because only through their own labor will he be able to achieve the same standard of living as his parents.
29:35 Well, yeah, capitalist brainwashing is definitely better than communist brainwashing, right?
31:15 If only he had read why the theorists wrote that, he would know that the renunciation of power would occur after the “withering away of the state.” For if there is no state, then what’s the point of any “party”?
35:29 The Romanovs were so “popular” among the peasants that, out of sheer love, they constantly rebelled against the ruling regime. And these small uprisings later, during the major redistribution of land within peasant communities every 12 years, escalated into full-scale revolutions in 1905 and 1917. That’s the kind of passionate love they had.
36:49 Its 100% lie. The royal family was shot by “moderate” socialists. They were in favor of continuing the war and wanted to use these murders to complicate peace negotiations with Kaiser’s Germany. And it was precisely on the orders of the local Council, which controlled by Socialist-Revolutionaries, that the entire family was shot. The Bolsheviks, however, were against the war, and Lenin sent a special commissar from the central government to the badly controlled region with the aim of transporting the Romanovs to Moscow—an effort that ultimately failed.
39:20 Throughout 1917–18, the Bolsheviks grew in popularity among the population month by month, eventually becoming the political majority. This is a historical fact. The majority of peasants supported the Bolsheviks and their reforms, particularly the land reform. And without the support of the peasants—who made up 85% of the population—the Bolsheviks would not have won the Civil War. The lecturer himself showed a map where 3/4 of the country’s territory was controlled by the Bolsheviks’ opponents.
39:55 Hahahaha, first he says that foreign banks didn’t give the Romanovs’ money to the Bolsheviks, and then he says that the banks did give money to the Bolsheviks.
Here’s another historical fact the lecturer doesn’t know: Latvian military units began to be formed in 1915. And after the February Revolution, just like in the rest of the russian army, soldiers’ councils began to form there. And surprise-surprise, the absolute majority of the council members supported the Bolsheviks even before they came to power. I don’t understand how the lecturer turned all this into mercenary activity.
45:35 Marx recognized Russia’s revolutionary potential. Much later, after the publication of the manifesto. Simply because he had expanded his knowledge (the lecturer should take a cue from this) in generaly and regarding Russia in particular. His correspondence with Lafargue and Vera Zasulich serves as confirmation. It’s just that at the time of the Manifesto, he was only 29 years old, and a deeper understanding of political-economic and historical processes came to him after he turned 50.
48:10 Mao became a communist in 1920 or 1921, even before the civil war in Russia had ended. And he was literally a nobody at that time.

Dude, the PRC is a socialist country, not a communist or capitalist one. The USSR was the Union of Soviet Socialist Republics, not communist republics. This isn't a no true Scotsman thing, rather a simple understanding of terms. Communists may form a party, and even govern a country, but they do so as a socialist project. That's why they don't say communist state, they say socialist state, because socialism is the path to communism. Communism is a moneyless, classless, stateless society. As far as I know, no one has ever claimed to have achieved nearly such a thing.

Also, Marx didn't say religion was the opiate of the masses to say it's evil. Read the full passage. His point was that it's a palliative, it treats the symptoms of the problems sometimes, but never treats the underlying illness. Not that it's flatly evil. For someone so educated, you seem to have never read Marx, or at least not understand it.

Again, Marx didn't invent communism. He did a lot of work to define and explain it's ideas, but he never worked alone, and he didn't get his ideas from nowhere. There are French, German, and English philosophy he was borrowing from, from Gerard Winstanley, to obviously Hegel, Adam Smith, Ricardo and other humanists and economists.

Professor knows a lot of many subjects, but communism is not one of them.

The theory that communism was created by English nobles to sabotage Germany is a conspiracy with no historical basis. Karl Marx lived in relative poverty in exile, supported by his work and by Friedrich Engels—there is not a single document or serious evidence of funding by elites. His critique of capitalism was universal, not a geopolitical weapon against Germany. The idea that the British elite would fund a movement advocating the overthrow of all monarchies and capitalism itself is illogical and ignorant of the historical context.


The claim that proletarian internationalism was a "trap" designed to fail also does not hold up. Marx advocated for the unity of workers as a natural response to the global expansion of capitalism. The practical difficulties of this unity—such as national and cultural differences—are real challenges for any transformative movement, not proof of sabotage. Marx dedicated his life to organizing the working class—his work and actions show genuine commitment, not a hidden plan to disrupt it. These theories distort basic historical facts and are not taken seriously by academia.

### **@oathboundsecrets** | 👍 2 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=axqDLhWs93Q&lc=UgwHgxdT1yrR7Jc0tVZ4AaABAg)

The Russian Civil War, Foreign Intervention, and the Myth of Wall Street Control

The Russian Revolution and Civil War (1917–1921) remain among the most dramatic political collapses in modern history. Because the period was chaotic, secretive, and violent, it has generated many theories — some supported by evidence, others not.

I. The Bolsheviks and War Communism (1918–1921)

After seizing power in October 1917, the Bolsheviks faced:
 • World War I collapse
 • Economic breakdown
 • Civil war against anti-Bolshevik forces
 • Foreign military intervention

To survive, they implemented War Communism, which included:
 • Nationalization of major industries
 • Abolition of private trade
 • Forced grain requisition from peasants
 • Centralized control of banks
 • Strict state distribution of goods

This resulted in:
 • Hyperinflation
 • Collapse of the monetary system
 • Urban depopulation
 • Severe famine

There was widespread confiscation of property belonging to nobles, clergy, merchants, and political opponents.

This is well documented in Soviet archives and mainstream scholarship.

II. The Red Terror

In 1918, following assassination attempts and internal unrest, the Bolsheviks launched the Red Terror:
 • Political repression through the Cheka (secret police)
 • Arrests and executions of perceived “class enemies”
 • Confiscation of property
 • Suppression of dissent

The Red Terror was a brutal consolidation of power. It did involve seizure of wealth from elites. However, historical evidence shows these resources were primarily used to sustain the regime and its war effort — not to finance foreign bankers.

III. Foreign Nationals in the Red Army

The Red Army included non-Russian fighters, most notably:
 • Latvian Riflemen (who played an important role in early Bolshevik security)
 • Former Austro-Hungarian POWs
 • Some Hungarians, Germans, and Chinese workers

However:
 • Most were stranded prisoners of war after World War I.
 • Many joined for ideological, political, or survival reasons.
 • They were not primarily mercenaries funded by foreign capital.

The claim that 200,000 foreign mercenaries were paid by Western financiers is not supported by mainstream academic research.

IV. Allied Intervention in the Russian Civil War

Several foreign powers intervened against the Bolsheviks:
 • Britain
 • France
 • United States
 • Japan
 • The Czechoslovak Legion

Their motivations included:
 • Preventing German access to Russian resources
 • Protecting Allied war supplies
 • Attempting to reopen the Eastern Front
 • Containing Bolshevism

However:
 • The intervention was limited and inconsistent.
 • Western governments were war-weary after World War I.
 • There was no unified strategy to restore the Tsar or decisively defeat the Bolsheviks.
 • Allied withdrawal occurred largely due to domestic political pressure and lack of commitment — not because Bolsheviks repaid debts.

V. Wall Street and the Bolsheviks

Wall Street and the Russian Revolution by Richard B. Spence examines financial interactions between Western bankers and revolutionary Russia.

Spence argues:
 • Some American and British financiers engaged pragmatically with Bolshevik authorities.
 • There were complex financial dealings involving gold and bonds.
 • Certain business interests sought opportunities amid chaos.

However:
 • There is no strong archival evidence that Washington financed the Bolshevik Revolution.
 • There is no proof that Wall Street orchestrated Russia’s collapse.
 • Western investors actually lost enormous sums when the Bolsheviks repudiated Tsarist debt in 1918.

If Western elites had intended to profit from the Revolution, the immediate outcome was largely financial loss.

VI. Confiscation and “Asset Stripping”

During War Communism:
 • Aristocratic estates were seized.
 • Church valuables were confiscated (notably in 1922).
 • Palaces and museums were appropriated by the state.
 • Banks were nationalized.

There was chaos, corruption, and some looting.

Estimates of hundreds of millions of dollars in seized valuables appear in historical discussions. However:
 • Most confiscated wealth remained under Soviet state control.
 • There is no reliable evidence that these assets were systematically transferred to Western financial institutions as repayment.

VII. What Can Be Concluded

Historically Supported
 • The Bolsheviks confiscated wealth.
 • The Red Terror involved repression and expropriation.
 • Foreign nations intervened in the Civil War.
 • Some Western financiers had limited financial dealings with the new regime.

Not Supported by Strong Evidence
 • That Wall Street engineered or financed the Bolshevik Revolution.
 • That Red Terror wealth was used to pay American bankers.
 • That 200,000 foreign mercenaries were funded by Western capital.

VIII. Philosophical Reflection: Revolution and Power

The Russian Civil War illustrates a recurring historical pattern:

Revolutions often dismantle existing elites — but replace them with new hierarchies.

The Bolsheviks destroyed:
 • Aristocratic privilege
 • Church authority
 • Private capital
 • Traditional social hierarchy

They created:
 • Party bureaucracy
 • Centralized state power
 • Political repression
 • A new ruling class

Rather than making Russia “amendable to capitalism,” the Bolsheviks constructed a state-controlled economic system that remained fundamentally anti-capitalist until reforms decades later.

Final Assessment

The Russian Civil War was primarily the result of:
 • World War I exhaustion
 • Economic collapse
 • Political radicalization
 • State breakdown
 • Ideological extremism

Foreign opportunism existed. Financial entanglements occurred. But the claim of a coordinated Western conspiracy to fund Bolshevism and destroy Russia is not supported by strong documentary evidence.

History is often tragic enough without adding hidden puppet masters.

### **@hernangb7717**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=axqDLhWs93Q&lc=UgzCt37CMoayv9VVnn14AaABAg)

George Orwell’s Animal Farm is a direct allegory of the Russian Revolution of 1917 and its aftermath. The overthrow of Mr. Jones mirrors the fall of Tsar Nicholas II, while Old Major represents Karl Marx and Lenin, whose ideas inspire revolution but don’t survive its outcome. The pigs, especially Napoleon, symbolize the Bolshevik leadership; Napoleon clearly representing Joseph Stalin while Snowball stands for Leon Trotsky, who is later expelled and erased from history. The gradual corruption of the farm shows how the revolution’s promise of equality turns into tyranny, illustrating Orwell’s warning that revolutions can replace one form of oppression with another when power becomes concentrated and unchecked.

### **@ebriadhlaceratus6609** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=axqDLhWs93Q&lc=UgyxZrcehwDgZVpE_EJ4AaABAg)

This is a really wild take that plays extremely loose with the definitions of these terms and is highly selective when emphasising certain similarities between capitalism and communism to make the argument they are aligned. It reminds me of similar attempts to equate communism and fascism. Personally I do not see modern China as proof of a false dialectic concerning capitalism and communism (the transition from capitalism to communism or vice versa has absolutely not been smooth in most other cases - the USSR, Romania, Cuba to take but a few examples), but a synthesis of these systems into something neither capitalist, nor communist in the traditional sense.
From what I hear, China takes some pains to mitigate the what it considers to be negative effects of its decision to embrace market economics and to maintain ideology rather than the interests of market actors (capitalists) as the guiding force of the nation - highly distinct from capitalist nations like the US in both scope and long-term intentions regarding government interference in markets.
If you are saying capitalism and communism both possess the characteristics of oligarchy, industrialisation and control over the minds of its citizens, then I would have no issue with this - and they are far from alone in these characteristics. There are enormous differences however.

### **@letranger4461** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=axqDLhWs93Q&lc=UgwPScCuFFBylWZxhct4AaABAg)

Look I'm not a historian. I took AP World a decade ago and occasionally read some popular history books. This has so many sweeping generalizations, strange assumptions, and just wrong things. I'm going to point out the first 4 that bother me most. I'll let other people debunk the stuff about Marxism and Communism, because I have never read any literature on it.

-	“Nationalism is the idea of loyalty to your people, loyalty to your nation. For capitalism, that's a problem. [...] Because capitalism wants to be loyal to capital and it wants capital to flow through across borders.”


 Ok, I get how that seems true given our current globalized economy built on free trade, but that is extremely recent. Capitalism does not exist without modern nation states, which required nationalism. They are a necessary precursor. Without a centralized state, who will enforce property laws? How do you have stable currencies without a centralized state? How do you have trust in a banking system? I recommend *_Debt: The First 5000 Years_* if you want a detailed history that isn't a textbook. Especially the chapters 8-12. He does cover things like economies and currency after the fall of the Western Roman Empire. 

-	“The idea of monarchy is that rather than power in the hands of a capitalist, power is in the hand of the king. Okay? And what a king often does is redistribute capital in a way that is more fair.” 


 No, they don’t. *Monarchy* is *inherently* *unfair* . Capitalism can and does coexist with monarchy. They are pretty compatible because capital tends to concentrate in fewer and fewer hands, under unfettered capitalism. That is extremely useful for monarchs because it *consolidates* *power* . A lot of monarchists who opposed the French Revolution were capitalists who believed in the free market and private property rights.


-	"All religion teaches you that money is evil" * 


Sir, *American* *Prosperity* *Gospel* has been one of the driving forces of U.S. Politics for, _at_ _least_ , the last 50 years. There are arguments about how theologically sound it is, but functionally, it is true for many people call themselves Evangelical Christians. If you consider historical religions, it’ even less true. If we’re talking about Catholicism, the restrictions around usury didn’t stop banking and lending. Those rules only applied to Christians. All economies above a certain scale require a banking system to keep track of credits and debts. Even feudal, subsistence economies. That’s why Jews are associated with money lending. Jews were ostracized and excluded from other trades in many parts of Europe. Judaism didn't explicitly condemn usury. Jews were already considered outsiders by Christians. Catholics may have condemned money lending, but _functionally_ it was allowed.



-	"That's why Catholic Europe was so poor"


Idk even know where to start. We know that's not true. It's not even a good generalization of Europe. Not only was the Church a major social safety net before modern states, but Catholic kingdoms were major economic powers well into the Modern period and so was the Church itself. The uptick in *economic* *productivity* , *in* *Europe* , starts two centuries before *the Reformation* even occurs. Maybe, it could be argued that tithing + taxes was _a_ _cause_ .
 It's still not even 10% of why. 

The *decline* *of* *Feudalism* , _two centuries earlier_ , was the major cause of higher economic productivity. That was *because* of *the* *Black* *Death* , which caused *labor* *shortages* , which led to peasants agitating for better conditions and higher wages, which also caused the decline because they could leave feudal manors for better opportunities in other towns which also encouraged *urbanization* . *The* *Feudal* *System* *was* *the* *major* *cause* *of* *poverty* . *Subsistence* *farming* *without* *any* *innovation* in the technology involved in farming was a cause. *Constant* warring and plundering by private armies was a cause. We haven't even touched the Crusades or the Little Ice Age.

### **@nyx4592** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=axqDLhWs93Q&lc=UgyruGMHWzAZT3Cgn8h4AaABAg)

TL:DR: The similarities between ideologies have more to do with the ideologies they oppose than them being close to each other.

Professor, allow me to respectfully disagree. Starting with the fact that many aspects of communism that you associate with Marx actually came from Lenin.

Monarchy and theocracy are systems that have existed for many millennia and it is anachronistic to think that Faroh and a French King at the height of absolutism are the same figure.

Absolutism arises in a very specific context where the French nobility is decimated in battle and the King accumulates many titles and attributions giving him ‘absolute power’. This centralisation gave France many advantages, especially militarily, which created a wave of absolutist governments across Europe to a greater or lesser degree. As this system wore out, various currents emerged that we understand as liberalism, which criticised the absolutist system. In France, the centralisation of the system generated a level of dissatisfaction that led to revolution. In Russia, which was more authoritarian than France, repression was enough to suppress revolutionary activity, while in several regions the end result was a compromise between the levels of power.

Later, socialist and nationalist movements would emerge that would oppose the outcome of the conflicts between nobiliary power and liberalism in the previous era.

It's important to remember that these conflicts were not just theoretical, but conflicts of interest between different classes: nobles, monarchs, bourgeoisie, peasants, proletarians, etc. In Scandinavia, for a number of reasons, rural producers have had greater autonomy for centuries. In France, absolutism allowed the king to impose his interests on the bourgeoisie. While in other countries the formation of a bourgeoisie would come from the nobility itself, not surprisingly in these places nationalism was born and became stronger against liberalism and socialism which had anti-aristocratic values and in the case of the latter anti-bourgeois as well. In the case of Marxism-Leninism, it was the result not only of centuries of Russian autocracy but also of the collapse of the empire and the refusal of governments along other lines to go to war, a pressure so great that it resulted in the most radical ideologies coming to power. Socialism and Communism have many strands beyond Marxism-Leninism that accept different levels of compromise.

Professor, you're wrong. Marx didn't talk about banning private property; he talked about abolishing private ownership of the means of production (factories, etc.). Marx didn't reject socialism; he spoke of socialism as a necessary stage in the construction of a communist society. If a state doesn't have socialized ownership of the means of production, that state can't be called socialist; such a state must have elements of a planned economy based on public ownership. You haven't read Karl Marx's works; I recommend you do.

### **@JamesOGant**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=axqDLhWs93Q&lc=UgxHCX_jX35FGkbJoe94AaABAg)

In the end everything is necessarily flawed, this is what makes the foil, the edge, the contrast useful for analysis, and also at the same time all these ideas will be misinterpreted and many false assumptions will be built in and people will argue with one another assuming that the other is using the same frame of reference, and the same assumptions or presumptions.  Chomsky talked about presumptions and how important they are.  

Marx said that religion was the evil of the world, but he was taken far too literally, and no one ever asked- what are the religions that hide in plain sight?  The worship of power and money and to some extent depravity and exploitation is what pervades all religions.  BUT at least actual religions in most cases have assumed that there is such a thing as good and evil...and there is such a thing as what is the right thing to do.   So they don't debate whether Jesus for example should have whipped the money changers in the temple.  They just say oh yeah of course he did, and it was the only time he was said to have been driven into a rage. ...

Anyways consider the idea of Liberalism and Liberty.  If it is the capacity for learning critical thinking, and the freedom to read and learn about  literature and art in all of its historical context where there are no sacred cows, where we can question everything in good faith with true intellectual honesty and curiosity then that is I think what is liberty and liberalism to some extent.  Sure people try to say that everything is all about "markets", but when we focus too much on money and the value of stuff being measured only in terms of money then we end up with a more neoliberal drift.  We end up with a world where no one can afford anything, no one is interested in reading anything, no one is interested in questioning anything and everyone is trapped in a cycle of consumerism, many are homeless, labor is not valued, the rent is too high, no one owns anything except for the extreme few who seem to own way too much.

And like in the US for example I can complain about this all day long, but the fact and reality of an unfree, antiliberal society will remain.  I can go and vote in the elections that they hold, but like George Carlin said the elections are just the illusion of choice, the popular vote is a minority consensus vote and the vote is being counted and managed in privately owned data black boxes.  And even if the cream was to rise to the top or some diamond was made in the rough and that savior like person rose to power, they would be quickly and easily coopted and blackmailed into doing many evil things which are detrimental to the many and to the whole group eventually.

This is the problem with greed culture and neoliberalism and shock doctrine.  The worship of evil is even bad for those who revel in and benefit from it, and I don't think it even creates diamonds from pressure.  It doesn't really even have a silver lining to it.  Because a lot of what is great about the world is often not even something we knew was there when it was there, and we have a hard time talking about it when it was gone, we pretend like it never existed or we didn't know we even had it until it was gone.  The spirit I guess and the culture is priceless when people are free and have liberty and liberalism- when they are free to have curiosity in good faith and are intellectually honest etc. 

When Karl Marx was talking about religion, maybe what he should have critiqued was magical thinking, and how powerful it is to have to meet people in the middle when they willfully or subconsciously take on a great many unnecessary or useless contradictions and false presumptions.  And that middle ground is always unfair- it trumps reason and understanding.  Especially when a great many people believe it.  And that is also what I think the greater meaning of the gospel is- it is ultimately an argument for agnosticism guided by good faith, trust in one another, and love for one another.

### **@weirdchamp7790**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=axqDLhWs93Q&lc=UgxcKjsRPTY0zV_m1qR4AaABAg)

1. The "Seamless" Transition in China
The Claim: China's transition from communism to capitalism was seamlessly executed, proving the ideologies are essentially the same.

The Facts: The transition was anything but seamless. Following the death of Mao Zedong, Deng Xiaoping’s economic reforms (starting in 1978) triggered massive internal political struggles, severe social upheaval, and widespread protests—most notably the 1989 Tiananmen Square protests. The shift dismantled the "iron rice bowl" (guaranteed state employment) and caused deep economic disparities. The Chinese Communist Party (CCP) maintained strict political control while integrating market economics, but the process was turbulent, violent, and fiercely contested.

2. Communism as a "Weapon" Created by Capitalism
The Claim: Capitalism engineered communism to destroy its true enemies: monarchy, religion, nationalism, and democracy.

The Facts: This reverses historical cause and effect. Marxism emerged in the 19th century as a direct critique of, and reaction to, the brutal working conditions of early industrial capitalism. While Marx indeed argued that communism would eventually abolish monarchy, organized religion, and bourgeois nationalism (viewing them as tools of class oppression), communism was not a "creation" of capitalists. It was an existential threat to them, leading to violent crackdowns on labor movements worldwide.

3. Karl Marx's Backing and Safe Haven in Britain
The Claim: It is suspicious that Marx was allowed to operate in capitalist Britain and had questionable financial backing.

The Facts: There is no paradox here, just historical irony. Marx fled to London in 1849 because he was exiled from Germany, France, and Belgium for inciting revolution. Britain at the time had the most liberal asylum and free-speech laws in Europe, making it a refuge for many political dissidents. Regarding his funding, Marx lived much of his life in crushing poverty. He was financially supported by his close collaborator, Friedrich Engels. Engels’ family owned textile mills, making him a wealthy capitalist. However, Engels used his personal wealth to support Marx out of deep, shared ideological conviction—not as part of a grand capitalist plot.

4. The Assassination of the Romanovs
The Claim: The Bolsheviks murdered the Russian Royal family to steal their fortunes in foreign capitalist banks.

The Facts: The Romanovs were executed in 1918 primarily for political and military reasons. The anti-Bolshevik "White Army" was advancing on Yekaterinburg, where the family was held captive. The Bolsheviks killed the Tsar and his family to prevent them from being rescued and used as a unifying symbol for the counter-revolution. While the Romanovs did have foreign assets, the Bolsheviks had no practical way of accessing them by simply murdering the family. In fact, most imperial assets held abroad were frozen by foreign governments and tied up in complex legal battles for decades.

5. Wall Street and the City of London Financed the Bolsheviks
The Claim: Western capitalists funded the Bolsheviks, enabling them to win the Russian Civil War via mercenaries and the "Red Terror."

The Facts: This is a well-known conspiracy theory popularized by writers like Antony Sutton in the 1970s. Prior to the revolution, the Bolsheviks funded themselves through party dues, bank robberies (notably organized by Stalin), and, most significantly, covert funding from the German Government during WWI (who sent Lenin back to Russia to destabilize it and knock Russia out of the war). While some Western businessmen sought lucrative trade deals with the Soviets after the revolution, Wall Street did not fund the Red Army. The Bolsheviks won the Civil War through mass conscription, controlling Russia's central industrial heartland, and using the Red Terror to violently crush internal dissent.

6. Capitalism and Communism as "Partners in World Conquest"
The Claim: The two systems are working in concert to reshape societies and exploit resources.

The Facts: Historically, the Cold War was a period of intense, genuine, and bloody geopolitical conflict. Millions died in proxy wars (e.g., Vietnam, Korea, Afghanistan) fought over these ideological divides. Framing them as secret partners is a variation of the "New World Order" conspiracy theory, which attempts to find a single, hidden hand behind all complex global events rather than accepting the messy reality of competing human ideologies

### **@ianstewart8243**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=axqDLhWs93Q&lc=UgzXhLf7rlY30FqSimN4AaABAg)

One thing that is never conversed about in these discussions (on line of off line), is the mental state of the individuals involved. People rail on about capitalism, but what is the common thread amongst those successful in that system? Control and greed and seemingly little care for the effects of their actions. 
If a more rational person was to grow in a capitalist system, would they be as greedy? Or would they do benevolent actions with their wealth? Same with communism. We look at history to see the effects on a population via communism. But what if the people running that system were more sane and rational than Stalin or Mao? What would happen?

A lot of hate gets directed at these systems and that we need to break them down and destroy them. The fact is, a system by itself does nothing. It needs people to make it run and the sanity of those people devising and running the system are the correct gauge as to how well it helps or hinders. Remove the people that harm through use or abuse of the system and put in place far more rational and pro survival people, you'd fine that things would improve in that system.

After the fall of the Tsars in 1917, power in Russia was taken over by the Bolsheviks, led by Vladimir Lenin, who established a Marxist–Leninist socialist state. While it’s true that some individuals in the late Russian Empire (including a few liberals and elites) had connections to Freemasonry, Freemasons as an organization did not take power in Russia.
In fact, once the Bolsheviks consolidated control, they banned Freemasonry, suppressed secret societies, and persecuted groups they saw as bourgeois or counter-revolutionary. The Soviet system was explicitly anti-mystical, anti-secret societies, and anti-elite networks.
The Tsars fell → the Bolsheviks took power → Freemasonry was outlawed, not the other way around.

### **@torinov2385**

The russian peasants didn't love the Romanov family at the time. Before certain events they were for the peasants a distant, God-ordained figure who would protect them from greedy landlords, officials, or nobles if only he knew their suffering. This idea stemmed from Orthodox Christian beliefs, autocratic traditions, and the fact that peasants were largely isolated in rural villages with limited education or contact with the wider world, but after Bloody Sunday (1905), World War I (1914–1917) and Rasputin scandal. By 1917, peasants were not actively "worshipping" the Romanovs. They showed a mix of indifference, contempt, or simmering anger. Many simply wanted more land (they already owned most of the arable land but craved the remaining noble estates) and relief from hardships.

### **@marlon-antonio-hallo** | 👍 1 likes

I loved all your lectures so far, but you should really take a closer look at Marxist theory. Most of these assumptions are just vulgar American conspiracy theories. Socialism doesn't mean redistribution, but to abolish the rent seeking class, that isn't productive, but uses its (inherited) capital to exploit workers. So Socialism means the actual realisation of "from each according to their ability, to each according to their work". This is also called the lower stage of communist society. Marx and Engels already anticipated that this will still not be the end of history because technology and productivity are constantly improving and at some point it would be possible to just give everybody what they need, because the amount of human work required to run society is so low, that people will just do it voluntarily without incentives. That higher stage is usually referred to as communism. But Marx and Engels never proposed to just jump from capitalism immediately to fully developed communism.

### **@jr.the.dude45**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=axqDLhWs93Q&lc=Ugwjxkw6JYnCyKIsgKV4AaABAg)

Jiang, you make a lot of arguments using the framework of a rich/middle/poor class framework, and I argue that you can not use this by itself to juxtapose communism against capitalism. The reason is that when you dig into what counts as a class, or where classes come from, you use a quantitative approach based loosely around the amount of things owned. What exactly counts as middle class, and how is it different from poor or rich? You can argue middle class is owning a 2 story house in america and the land it sits on, but is this the same expectation that can be had of other countries? is it universal? Is a man who has a million dollars yet owns nothing else and chooses to live like Diogenes poor/middle/rich?  It should be clear that this approach is actually quite subjective, and will lead to distortions in your analysis. A better framework which can be applied universally without leaning on subjective quantification lies in the categorization of *how* you make your money. Do you trade your time for it (as with wages) or do you passively accumulate it through the benefits of ownership? You can only ever make it yourself or have others make it for you, and this is why the alternative framework is between the owning class and the working class (and your version of middle class becomes one who both owns and works, usually out of necessity, which simplifies into working class). If you want to do a dialectic analysis between the two ideologies, it is necessary to not just use the classical capitalist understand of class, but the marxist understanding as well.
