Chatgpt

Did Marx originate anything in The Communist Manifesto?

Most of what Marx wrote in the Manifesto builds on preexisting ideas, especially those from:

Georg Wilhelm Friedrich Hegel: Marx’s entire dialectical method is a materialist inversion of Hegel's idealist dialectic. The idea of historical development through contradiction (thesis-antithesis-synthesis) is straight out of Hegel.

Adam Weishaupt and the Illuminist tradition: The Illuminati promoted the destruction of monarchies, private property, and organized religion—core themes echoed in Marx's writing, especially the abolition of the bourgeois family and religion.

French Revolutionaries and Utopian Socialists: Thinkers like Babeuf, Saint-Simon, and Fourier all advocated forms of collectivism, redistribution of wealth, and the classless society before Marx.

### **@canalmultipolartv** | 👍 2 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=axqDLhWs93Q&lc=Ugy0p-OmrH9UctOZXvN4AaABAg)

Five minutes of video, and it becomes clear what the indiscriminate use of Game Theory leads to. It pushes its users to see everything in black and white. On the other hand, this Manichean superficiality leads to the disregard of factors hidden beneath the appearance of phenomena.



It's hard to believe the author made such errors after so many classes teaching not to do so. Without prejudice to a React video (and perhaps an article) I will make on @canalmultipolartv, I will state from the outset that the analysis fails, at the very least and to start with, in the following conceptual fallacies:



Lack of understanding of the concept of "mode of production" and the distinction between "mode of production" and the resulting politico-social superstructure, as well as the role the mode of production plays in social construction.



Unaware of the difference between Marxism and Leninism and the role of Lenin and Leninism in the political organization of a society transitioning from capitalism (in the Russian case, still largely feudal) to socialism.



The role of the "communist party" as an instrument and not an end in itself, and as an instrument for defending a transition, which may later even dispense with its own existence (Lenin even speaks of dispensing with the state itself once class contradictions are overcome);



Capitalism as the antithesis of Communism only exists in the author's head; at most, for Marx, the antithesis of capitalism is socialism, with communism being a synthesis, in the Hegelian sense, of the confrontation between two transitional modes of production (for Marx, Capitalism and Socialism are transitional modes of production, while communism is terminal); Communism is the result of "overcoming" [Aufhebung], meaning it is "superior," just as socialism is the result of "overcoming" capitalism;



The author is thus unaware of materialist dialectics and the role of historical materialism, that is, the way modes of production succeed each other as a result of their internal and external contradictions, overcoming themselves in new, historical (i.e., resulting from the material conditions of a specific era, historical phase), transient modes of production, with superior modes prevailing over the overcome ones;



The author is also unaware that for Marx, each material context is historical, meaning it is transient, and therefore, the very existence of a vanguard, of a communist party, is also historical, existing only while material reality demands it, extinguishing itself, like everything in nature, when it is no longer needed; hence, each material context is specific to the reality in which it exists: there is not one single communism, just as there is not one single feudalism, one single capitalism...



Unaware of Marx's life, Marxism, and that Marx spoke very little of "communism." Marx spoke mainly about capitalism, so that it could be known and understood, so that, gaining consciousness, the proletariat could use its contradictions to overcome it;



Also unaware that for Marx, capitalism is an evolution, that modes of production evolve by overcoming themselves, but, to the extent that some are syntheses of the contradictions of previous ones ("synthesis" and not "contradiction"), they retain remnants of the previous ones, meaning it is a construction by addition and substitution, and not merely by exclusion.



These are some profound contradictions that are serious and demonstrate grave failures in the structure of thought (even a lack of understanding of how dialectics works). But in the superficial contradictions, the matter is also serious:



a) Marx did not invent "communism"; communism is an ancient human aspiration, dating back to prehistoric humans and means "living in community, sharing everything, including power and property." Examples of what can be communism (humanity only lived in communism in its primitive form (and not "socialism" as the author says), meaning without consciousness of doing it). Thomas More in Utopia, Jesus Christ, and many others before Marx established principles and functioning that we could consider "communist," in light of progressive political thought;

b) The author is unaware of the difference between "scientific socialism" and "utopian socialism," hence the confusion regarding Marx. Marx founded the scientific socialist current, meaning the one that would use the most advanced scientific knowledge to accelerate the overcoming of capitalism and arrive more quickly at socialism and then communism. Superficially speaking (it's much more than this).

c) The author says Marx was an instrument of capitalism because England was "the most capitalist" and let him live there. Unaware that Marx also lived in Germany and was only expelled because he did not follow the imposed rules. But, if England was "the most capitalist," what about it being a Monarchy, which the author says is "an enemy of capitalism"? And what about the USA, which in the 20th century are the most capitalist, and imprisoned all communists? Or that 20th-century fascism was based on private capitalist monopolies, and imprisoned communists? Or that, in the most capitalist era of all, the present one, many socialist parties govern, not just being tolerated (like some communists), but supported by capitalism? Just to directly attack some of the author's superficial hypotheses.

d) The author is unaware of the relationship between "mode of production," the politico-social superstructure that protects it, and how these transition within modes of production. Constitutional monarchy is an adaptation to the capitalist mode of production. Therefore, we can have capitalism and monarchy and, in theory, even socialism and monarchy.

e) The author is unaware of the difference between nationalism and patriotism and internationalism. Internationalism means "friendship between peoples, between workers" and not "abolition of borders." This utopian vision was never Marx's. There are no countries more sovereigntist than Cuba or North Korea, governed by communist parties, just like China. The role of the Communist Party in those countries is to ensure the evolutionary process towards socialism is carried out; they exist to defend and lead that trajectory, containing the political power of capitalism. In China, this capitalist power is contained through CPC committees in companies, public ownership of strategic sectors, etc... And it is also necessary to distinguish between "private property" and "capitalism," as they are not the same thing.

f) In socialism, "democracy" is not achieved through voting, but through daily participation in political life. Socialist society is activated through social structures (unions, associations, committees (like soviets), cooperatives...), which have elected representatives and have political power. They are forms of socialization of property. Consider Huawei, impossible in a "capitalist" country, meaning a country where political initiative comes from capital and not from the worker). In capitalism, you vote every 4 years, in elections paid for by capitalism, to legitimize it.

g) The author is unaware that democratic construction, like socialism, is very diverse, with many models and practices. In the case of Religion, what about Cuba? A very religious society, governed by a communist party? What about the Cuban constitution being the only one in the world that recognizes the right to change one's sex?

h) The author is unaware that Marx died in misery and had two children die of hunger, being very poorly treated by the "capitalism" he supposedly served, and that Engels was disinherited by his family. And Lenin who exiled himself in Germany and not England?

i) Unaware that in socialism (socialization of property) and in "communism" (collectivization of property), democracy is presumed; for Marx, the superior form of democracy is economic, and that is measured by the amount of wealth that goes to the working people. The levels of redistribution and wealth in the USSR were enormous and the social system was very good. Unions managed pensions, social security. The prices of fundamental things were very cheap. Public services in Vietnam, Cuba, China, Korea are very cheap. Because they are rights and not meant for accumulation.

j) And here the author shows a lack of understanding of the function of property for Marx. Property has a social purpose, meaning to serve society. In capitalism, property has an economic purpose, of accumulation. The state exists to protect that accumulation, whether in its nationalist, globalist, monarchical, or theocratic form, as in Saudi Arabia. The socialist or communist state (there hasn't been one yet) aims to protect distribution and the social purpose of property. Since everything revolves around satisfying material needs, that satisfaction guaranteed by the superstructure (another concept the author is unaware of) is the superior form of democracy.



I caught all this in five minutes... What a disappointment with this author!

### **@anthonyewolf** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=axqDLhWs93Q&lc=UgyNYjwFGB_IIs3a5ct4AaABAg)

I tend to reject views of Engels creating funding for communist thinking as an intentional way to destroy communism. It paints too broad of a brush over individual conflicts that were well recorded in Engels' life and presents the illusion that the world was created this way by a group of capitalists who were at war with other ideologies and doesn't explain why Engels would want to spread "the virus" well past his life time. Rather these are phenomena created out of the way that other institutions also supported capitalism at the time: the church and political figures also needed money and partners to maintain power. It is easier to conflate war against traditional ideas with continued vanguardism of marxist-leninism than it is to believe in a conspiracy among capitalists. At best, the clarification at the end of the lecture (51:00) explains the use of the red scare to attack communism and drive the west into conflating a mixed economy with a command economy. It's also convenient to say the soviet union was amenable to fall to capitalism by discarding its traditions while leaving out the effects of the war in Iran, the shortages in resources as military tension built up, and the relaxations in media that allowed criticisms of the command economy to surface. 

More interesting is to see how materialism, theocracy, democracy, and nationalism are lenses to analyze the public consciousness. I am appreciative of the way that is applied in this video.

>
> ​@hajacnl008 This video contains so much misinfo that i think it would require an entire second video to go through them all, but just at face value, here are some of the things that are egregiously untrue or misframed by the prof:  
>   
> Communism is summarily a stateless, moneyless, classless society. That is diametrically opposed to capitalism at a foundational level. Methodologically, proposed transitions to communism, largely embodied through Socialist Policy, may not exhibit this polarity, but it doesn't change the fact that asserting them as compatible is just an untrue statement.  
>   
> China's transition to a hybridized capitalist system at the behest of Xiaoping was considered a tool of necessity (despite criticism by many communists to this day) in order to build stability, especially wrt to US-employed economic sanctions and food shortages. It only makes sense that the transition was seamless on account of the CCP's lineage of Marxism-Leninism, enacting a variant of what Lenin himself called state capitalism. The same government enacted this change – there was no need for a violent transition back to capitalism.  
>   
> Communism being created by capitalism as a means of destroying capitalism's enemies doesn't hold water on so many levels, but for starters, communism predates capitalism by millennia (what Marx called primitive communism, though this is likely a misnomer bred from Enlightenment-derived racism). Furthermore, capitalist societies have been violently anti-communist since communism became a threat to ruling class hegemony. The death toll of communists and anarchists from anti-communist violence is literally countless. Deaths from the Holocaust? Economic sanctions? Red Scare? McCarthyism? Numerous executions throughout Europe and the Global South? Massacres in the US? CIA-backed coups? That doesn't come from an economic system that considers the Left a tool to maintain power.  
>   
> Religion is not an enemy of capitalism – like you've even stated in previous lectures, the propagation of monotheism is one of societal control – a tool that places money and individualism (two guiding facets of capitalist ideology) at the forefront of guiding principles for humanity. It is, in fact, extremely effective in the upkeep of power because God can be retooled at the whim of the state. The same goes for nationalism, a tool of the nation-state. While it contradictorily has been used as an anti-capitalist tool in the past (China itself and Korean anarchism being stand-out examples), it lends itself to exceptionalism, which aligns itself neatly with capitalism's tools of control. I highly recommend Fredy Perlman's The Continuing Appeal of Nationalism to learn more about how it came to be as a concept and how it's been used to instigate wars and violent forms of bigotry (all of which are respectively used to maintain power). The bourgeoisie are going to flow capital across borders regardless of prevailing sentiment over national pride.  
>   
> Socialism is a dictatorship of the proletariat – the workers own the means of production.  
>   
> Communism does not work with capitalism to destroy these things. It is opposed to capitalism, which upholds these facets – among others – as tools to maintain its status quo (arguably, communists are not even monolithically committed to ending religion). Monarchism is a separate ideology in conflict with both capitalism and communism.  
>   
> You do understand what a vanguard party is (specifically this was a concept put forth by Lenin well after communism was given academic context but I digress), but it is not tantamount to an oligarchy. An example of an oligarchy would be a wealthy elite, not a handful of bureaucrats reorganizing a centralized economy.  
>   
> The rich paying more taxes does not equate to socialism. If anything, it's a singular facet of social democracy (which actually has something resembling democracy, though historically even that isn't always true).  
>   
> “Religion is the opiate of the masses” is not claiming that religion is evil. In fact, Marx was softer on religion than many of his contemporaries – especially compared to the likes of Stirner.  
>   
> Marx was not the founder of communism. He even acknowledges in his writing that not only did he not come up with the idea, he didn't even put a name to it. He even acknowledges that it existed in other forms long before the term originated.  
>   
> 19th century Britain was a haven for protecting free speech and press; it wasn't uncommon for political exiles to end up there, Marx included. Despite not imposing censorship, Marx was not totally uncontested by British authorities. Marx earned most of his income through commercial publication. Surely, his books and translations made no money; his journalism did. Also, as you speculated, he did have several patrons. While Engels was one of these, he was not so at the behest of his father – a wealthy industrialist who correctly considered Engels a class traitor.  
>   
> Socialism was by no means the dominant ideology, though it was popular amongst the working class of industrial Europe due to observable exploitation under capitalism.

### **@Zaboadikaza**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=axqDLhWs93Q&lc=UgyzvFQfHGf_8g9xwf14AaABAg)

I think he makes a lot for errors based of lack of knowlegde in some crucial areas for this subject, which causes him trickle down on these errors through confirmations biases, which causes this theory to not only lack aknowlegdement of any accurate depictions of any context or global perspective, but also contradict some of his better lectures that does not look at capitalisme or communisme in isolation.

Speaking from European sitting in one of the most prosperous countries.
Do not conflate socialism with social democracy.

Socialism: is about reducing or removing private ownership in favor of the workers and/or state to governe it - to compare any situation to socialism, that country would have had all of their main infrastructure controlled by the workers (not just as employees but decision makers). Name me one country where this is the case. 

Social Democracy: is about the state (within an democratic framework) involving themselves in the leverage of power between the Work unions and the "Job Provider Coalitions" in order to prioritize the countenous production of society in favor our "market-democracy".

The greater equality in Europe and especially in the Nordics: are a result of gaining economic leverage after a transition from Monarcy, before capitalism could fully take root.
This has resulted in a situation where we have seen the social safetynet get depleted (not followed GDP) since the 1970'.
This means the Social Democracy --> (Neo) Leberalism --> Capitalisme, as the workers do not own/control the modes of production, which is what Karl Marx believed what needed to get Socialism or Communism.
Marx was wrong about some stuff though: Like his about a natural evolution from Monarcy to Capitalism (true), but also natural evolution from Capitalism to Socialism (False).

Richard B. Spence is not an amateur; he is a trained academic historian with the access and skills to research primary archives.

• Primary Sources: Spence’s work is indeed rich in documentation. He utilizes US State Department archives, military intelligence records, private banker archives, and diplomatic documents from the era.

• The Interpretative Leap: Where the documentary base becomes controversial is in the interpretation. Spence (and other authors in this vein, like Antony Sutton) often presents documents proving commercial transactions or contacts between Western bankers and revolutionaries, and interprets these contacts as deliberate ideological or conspiratorial support to create communism.

• Nature of the Evidence: Much of the evidence is circumstantial. The fact that a banker transferred gold to the Bolsheviks can be documented, but the motivation—whether it was a conspiracy to control Russia or simply opportunistic short-term profit—is where the author’s speculation comes in.



THIS THESIS IS WIDLY REJECTED BY ITS PAIRS or viewed with skepticism by the actual historiography.

Most academic historians specializing in the Russian Revolution do not accept the central premise that Wall Street was a driver of the revolution. The main criticisms revolve around three points:

• Opportunism vs. Conspiracy: Peers criticize Spence for confusing "profit" with "conspiracy." The standard academic view is that bankers (such as those at Guaranty Trust or J.P. Morgan) were opportunists trying to make money with whichever government took power in Russia, be it the Tsar, Kerensky, or Lenin. They were "hedging" their bets, not engaging in social engineering.

• Reductionism: Historians criticize the idea that external forces (American money) were decisive. Modern historiography focuses on Russia's internal social pressures, famine, war, and mass politics as the real causes of the revolution, viewing external financing as secondary.

• The Publisher: The book Wall Street and the Russian Revolution was published by Trine Day, a publisher specifically known for books on conspiracy theories, the occult, and "suppressed history." This alone places the work outside the circuit of major university presses (like Oxford or Cambridge), signaling that the book likely did not undergo the same rigorous blind peer review process as standard academic works.

3. Comparison with Antony Sutton

It is impossible to discuss Spence without mentioning Antony Sutton, who wrote Wall Street and the Bolshevik Revolution in the 1970s.

• Spence updates Sutton’s thesis.

• While Sutton is often dismissed by academia as a rigid conspiracy theorist, Spence presents a more academic and nuanced guise, focusing heavily on espionage.

• Spence is a specialist in the history of espionage (he wrote about the spy Sidney Reilly), and his peers tend to respect his work on espionage techniques more than his macroeconomic conclusions regarding Wall Street.

im at 10 minutes and im already crashing out. ... honest question: is this satire???

1. Communism IS NOT what is explained here. Hes basically saying communism is what historic so called communist countrys did. communism IS NOT the "party" and even if it was, the party IS NOT oligarcy.
per definition communism is the evolution of socialism. in communist theory, there is no money, no power, no government in communism, therefore, there is no party.
2. socialism = democracy. oh lord where do i even beginn.... to me this rieks of the typical american view on european social democracies. for fucks sake get this in your head: social democracy IS NOT socialism. Socialism per definition means no accumaulation of wealth, no private ownership of the means of production and democratic principles in the work place. it IS NOT democracy. Democracy is a way of governing, socialism is an economic model. they can go hand in hand, but they are notably not the same thing. even though i live in a social democracy, i still dont have any say in what my company does. i enjoy universal healthcare and a social safety net but this is not socialism for fucks sake!
3. marx was a capitalist bc he lived a wealthy life.... Marx came from a well off family and married into a wealthy one, yes that much is true. but due to him being a writer in victorian england, he had no stable income and lived off inheritans and small publications. the victorian system was status driven. working people couldnt get a seat in any library let alone a meeting with a publisher. being somewhat "middle class" was a necessity to even be able to publish i.e. get any influence for his cause. marx struggled his whole life to keep this middle class fassade upright, he was absolutely not wealthy in andy way. engels knew that and financed marx out of principle. engels send marx money through letters his father couldnt really stop. Engels father knew about his sons worldviews and tried to correct him by making him work in his factory with the outspoken aim to rehabilitate his son. and of cause engels was paid for his work, but mentioned countless times that he also hated it. as far as history is concerned, engels father didnt willingly finance marx, this was hidden from him. 
4. funny how socialism is democracy and from 12:00 onwards it is the natural evolution of capitalism because of 'consolidation of capital' ... this is half right. socialism in theory emerges when the living conditions of the workers under capitalism become unbearable. half right bc thats what happens when capital consolidates, but its also only half of the true picture. 
5. fuck im getting tierd of this ... 'socialism is the redistribution of wealth from the rich to the poor, therefore the middle class becomes the new rich class'. fml.... at most socialism is the equal redistribution of wealth. the middle class isnt suddenly the class with the most money. in socialism capital accumulation is not possible, there is no longer a wealthy class to begin with god damit! you own what you need to live but you cant own 10 houses to rent and get passive income for example. 

i cant ... i just cant ... go read some actual theory, or do like he did and huff some glue for all i care...

### **@fatimab5561-w7c**

From the videos you’ve posted, it’s pretty clear what your worldview is and where you stand. The tone and narrative in your content say a lot. To many people, it looks like you’re repeating the same talking points that authoritarian systems and their supporters have been pushing for years. ( COMMUNISME 💨) 

But the reality today is different. People around the world are much more aware than before. They recognize propaganda, and they can see when someone is trying to distort what’s happening in order to discourage people who are pushing for change.

If you think that negative predictions or attempts to discredit a movement will weaken people’s determination, you’re probably misunderstanding the moment we’re living in. History has shown again and again that when people demand change, those movements can grow stronger despite pressure, fear, or outside narratives trying to undermine them.The world is changing, and many of the old political games that powerful states used for influence and leverage are becoming more visible to everyone. People remember who stood for freedom and who tried to protect failing systems.
Maybe before making such confident claims about the future, it would help to spend some time studying history more carefully.

从你发布的视频来看，你的世界观和立场其实已经非常明显。你内容里的语气和叙事方式，让很多人觉得你只是在重复一些长期以来由某些威权体系支持者传播的说法。

但今天的现实已经不一样了。世界各地的人们比过去更加清醒，也更容易识别宣传和误导。当有人试图通过扭曲事实、制造悲观情绪来打击那些希望改变现状的人时，很多人其实都能看得出来。
如果你认为通过这些预测或者负面的描述就能削弱人们追求改变的决心，那可能是对当前时代的一种误判。历史已经多次证明，当社会中的人们真正希望改变时，这种力量往往会越来越强。
世界正在发生变化。过去一些大国通过影响和操控他国所进行的政治游戏，也越来越被人们看清。历史最终会记住谁站在自由的一边，谁选择维护正在衰落的体系。
在对未来做出如此确定的判断之前，也许更值得先认真研究一下历史。

السلام عليكم
May the peace, blessings, and mercy of God be upon you

May Allah guide us to the straight path.

My brothers, we are all children of our father Adam.

Why is Islam the true religion?
Because Islam is the only religion on the face of the earth whose truth can still be verified until today.

Every prophet was sent with a miracle. Prophet Ibrahim entered the fire and came out unharmed. Prophet Musa split the sea with his staff. Prophet Isa brought the dead back to life by Allah’s permission. And Prophet Muhammad also performed miracles, such as the splitting of the moon.

But all of these miracles were witnessed only by the people who lived at the time of those prophets.

So why Islam?
Because Allah chose to reveal the Qur’an to Prophet Muhammad and took upon Himself the preservation of the Qur’an, while the Torah and the Gospel were entrusted to people for preservation. That is why the Torah and the Gospel were altered, but the Qur’an was not.

So what proves that the Qur’an was not altered?
This is the miracle within it from the Lord of the worlds, for all times.

First, the Arabic language, which is one of the richest languages. Until today, the Qur’an is preserved in the hearts of millions of Muslims before it is preserved in books, because it is memorized orally, not just written.

This language and its miraculous style can be understood by Arabs. And what about non-Arabs?
Here is where it becomes clear: when Muslims developed sciences such as mathematics, astronomy, and medicine, and after the decline of Islamic civilization and the fall of the Caliphate, these sciences were transferred to the West. Then discoveries began to appear over the last hundred years after the Islamic Caliphate. With these discoveries came scientific confirmations, from non-Muslims, of verses mentioned in the Qur’an 1,400 years ago—things that neither Prophet Muhammad nor his companions had knowledge of.

Here is your role, O non-Muslim or skeptic: read the Qur’an and see Allah speaking to your mind, saying, “I am the One who created you and created the universe. These are My signs and proofs for you so that you may believe in Me without seeing Me, but you will see My signs everywhere—in yourself, in your life, and in the universe.”

For example:
The Qur’an speaks about bees and honey.
It speaks about ants communicating and being crushed rather than simply dying.
It says that Allah made from water every living thing.
It speaks about the stages of the embryo in the mother’s womb and that the man determines whether the child is male or female.
It speaks about the barrier between two seas.
And much more.

Today, there is also discussion about numerical miracles in the Qur’an:
The number of times Paradise and Hell are mentioned,
Man and woman,
Angels and devils,
The word “day” mentioned 360 times,
The word “month” mentioned 12 times,
And many more.

How could an unlettered man who could neither read nor write recite this Qur’an to people, revealed over 23 years, under persecution and war, and say all of this?
A language so eloquent that it challenged all Arabs in their own tongue.
Scientific facts mentioned 1,400 years before their discovery.

O Allah, our Lord, guide our hearts to You, Lord of the worlds.
I hope that anyone who reads these words reflects upon them and realizes that after death there is Paradise and Hell, and this matter is not a joke. You must know your Creator before you say on the Day of Truth, “If only I had known.”

Really weak analysis on this one. I was wondering if this channel was ever going to allude to any sort of political perspective or project, but this video reveals that Jiang's understanding of economics and politics is kind of incoherent. It's disappointing for me because I find his talks about anthropology, religion, and eschatology to be extremely compelling (and seemingly inspired by David Graeber which gave confidence). I'm all for a counter-narrative (even with occasional elements of conspiracy theories), but this argument is premised on a lot of false information and demonstrates very confused interpretation of the subject. 

Communism is the destruction of property? What?

Yes, game theory is a valuable tool, but if you simply take the current situation and back-solve into a grand narrative where each event is a coherent and intentional move by the winning player, then you are always going to end up with a conspiratorial mess. It's a bit of a stretch to call that game theory. And the "Guys, I'm just speculating -- don't take me seriously" is getting old. Give solid and well supported analysis or disclose the shortcomings of your counter-narrative.

Ironically, having an understanding of dialectics would probably enhance his ability to see which way the winds are blowing on the geopolitical stage.

### **@bebop.**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=axqDLhWs93Q&lc=Ugxejq7x3KWRH47_X2l4AaABAg)

(I'm not even a Marxist) I'm sorry but this is wrong. Socialism and communism had been more or less synonymous when Marx was writing. Marx obviously didn't invent communism or socialism, although he seems to have popularized the word "communism". (The ideological distinction between communism and socialism is largely due to Lenin, who sees socialism as the necessary first step to communism. Incidentally, the vanguard party idea discussed here is also by Lenin...) 

The views of Marx himself were not very different from the prevailing socialist thought at the time. Yes he was an activist and a revolutionary, but he wasn't particularly more revolutionary than every other socialist at the time. His most famous work Communist Manifesto is not a particularly ground breaking (besides being exceptionally well written), it is the manifesto of political party they were founding (when Marx was 30). 

What Marx did that was special is in etheory of economics, that in Das Capital he provided the most through analysis of capitalism since Adam Smith . But it is a dry economics treatise, not at all a firebrand guide to revolution.

>

> Speaking of materialism, Marx of course didn't invent materialism per se. His materialism is in the context of Hegelianism, and he may be the first materialist Hegelian (if not the first he is definitely the most important). But that doesn't necessarily have that much to do with his left politics, there are left and right Hegelians. (Materialist dialectics is more of a philosophical analysis method, so you can technically be a Marxist without being a socialist, but that is a very technical point.)  
>   
> In any case, the video is incorrect, because it attributes a lot of the ideas Lenin introduced to Marx, to make a point that Marx invented "communism" to dicredit the socialist movements by introducing revolutionary radicalism. This is simply not correct, historically.

### **@TonyMenchProductions**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=axqDLhWs93Q&lc=Ugxh6ajK3bIrWaqpObd4AaABAg)

Me he decepcionado bastante con el video del prof Jiang. Se ve que no tiene ni  idea de lo que es comunismo... si realmente supiera no hubiera comenzado con la premisa de desmentir que son opuestos con el enfoque erroneo que le esta dando. Algunas incongruencias que encontré en 10 minutos de video:
1. Marx en ningun momento dice que comunismo y capitalismo son antagonicos, el comunismo es una transicion post capitalista pasando por el socialismo primeramente. Donde el fin es eliminar al estado y que no haya clases sociales.
2. Comparar mercado con capitalismo es un error muy comun, el mercado ha existido siglos antes del surgimiento del capitalismo. El capitalismo es un modelo economico donde los medios de produccion son privados.
3. Decir que China es "hipercapitalista" es una falacia. China es socialista con caracteristicas chinas. Tomando en cuenta mi punto uno donde hablo sobre la transicion hacia el comunismo, hay que agregar que el PCC es propietario/y o forma parte de la mayoria de las empresas. Tiene planes economicos donde controla al mercado y los sectores clave de desarrollo. En China el estado es propietario de la tierra, no hay inversiones ni especulacion. Lo menos que podria definir a China eses un modelo "hipercapitalista"; ese seria el caso de USA, donde ni el estado puede ofrecer salud publica. Para ser capitalista los medios de produccion deben de ser privados. No es el caso en China, donde muchas empresas no lo son.

MEGA FALACIA!
Me sorprende de un chino-canadiense.

### **@pleiades_community**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=axqDLhWs93Q&lc=UgzkI7exhACqLGgBXsl4AaABAg)

No, professor. Capitalism - is a stage of development communism. But not the same. And capitalism didnt create it
The idea of building communism after capitalism is part of Marxist theory, which posits that communism is an inevitable historical stage arising from the contradictions of capitalism. The founders of Marxism—Karl Marx and Friedrich Engels—developed a theory that includes a revolutionary path, the abolition of private property, and a transition to collective ownership.

Some key tenets of Marxist theory on communism after capitalism:

- The contradictions of capitalism (concentration of capital, crises of overproduction, growing social stratification) cannot be resolved within its own framework. According to Marx, communism is the only system capable of resolving these contradictions.
- The development of productive forces under capitalism creates the material base for a higher level of social production, which will form the foundation of communist society.
- The proletarian revolution—the proletariat becomes aware of its exploitation, unites, and overthrows the capitalists through a socialist revolution.
- The transitional period from capitalism to socialism—a special historical phase beginning with the working class seizing political power and ending with the construction of socialism, the first phase of communist society.
- The higher phase of communism—ownership of the means of production becomes collective, belonging to all members of society rather than the state.
- The classics of Marxism did not claim to provide a detailed picture of the communist future—in their view, communism is an open prospect, a natural-historical direction of civilization's development, rather than a completed ideal state.

### **@pumpkaraoke** | 👍 2 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=axqDLhWs93Q&lc=UgxNtUOXW2hGslejJHB4AaABAg)

Almost none of what Jiang says in this video is true. The central issue is that he doesn’t seem to know anything about capitalism, socialism, communism, Marx, Engels or the 19th century. And, like many of the concepts he uses throughout his videos, he refuses to define anything. Even if his definitions are wrong, at least we could see where he goes wrong or why he makes the arguments he makes. But Jiang skips quickly over any subtlety that prevents him from making grand proclamations about the nature of the world.

What’s particularly frustrating about Jiang here, is that he refuses to engage with the real history of the intellectuals who worked tirelessly to build a theory of human emancipation. You might disagree with Marx, Engels, Lenin etc., but the idea that their lives were dedicated to some sort of ideological trick to allow the capitalists to take over the world is absurd. Lenin’s brother was hanged by the Czar, and he spent his life building an intellectual foundation for a revolution that quickly destroyed his body once it succeeded. Engels spent countless hours over years tirelessly documenting the inhumane torture of the working people of England by the capitalists and was one of the first a few who ever did so. Marx was in England precisely because he was *studying capitalism* and he’d been deported from Prussia, and then France, and then Belgium for fomenting revolution and refusing to succumb to censorship. He was poor most of his life and was harangued by countless creditors he bamboozled in order to continue his work. His daughter Laura believed so completely in socialism that her and her husband, both revolutionaries, killed themselves in their late 60s, so that resources that might have gone to them in their old age would otherwise go to the socialist cause.

And, again, maybe you disagree with all of this. I clearly don’t, but that’s fine. But to offhandedly trash the lives of some of the most decent, morally committed people in history without any evidence whatsoever is a disgusting travesty.
